HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  5
Introduction
What is the purpose of this document?
This document is the scientific framework for the HAS’ multi-year work programme entitled: “Helping social and socio-medical facilities and services gathering the perspective of the people they support”. It will be transposed into different formats in order to make it accessible to as many people as possible. As the HAS work programme advances, it will beenhanced with deliverables aimed at practical and organizational purposes.
It is aimed at:
‒ The professionals
1
 and governing bodies that support people in social and medico-social ser-vices and facilities (ESSMS);
‒ Supported persons so that they can be fully involved in the processes designed to gather their perspective;
‒ Any other person or institution involved in this type of process.
2
This document aims to provide the essential keys required to support meaningful collection and con-sideration of the perspectives
3
 of supported persons, by:
‒ Proposing a process that is meaningful for everyone and avoids any stigmatisation of or dis-crimination against the various people involved. This process invites those involved to:
• Consider and question, rather than setting a predetermined framework;
• Combine different perspectives to improve the quality of life of both the supported persons themselves and their professional and personal entourage;
‒ Demonstrating the multiple advantages of collecting and considering the perspective of sup-ported persons, for themselves, their families, professionals, governing bodies and the authori-ties responsible for pricing and controls. Thereby highlighting certain watch-points to be taken into account;
‒ Presenting accessible, realistic methods and a wide range of practical, illustrated resources so that everyone can become familiar with and take ownership of the elements in order to be ac-tively involved throughout the process.
4
How was this document constructed?
The document was developed based on a detailed analysis of the scientific literature.
This process was carried out with an expert working group (supported persons, peer supporters, pro-fessionals and researchers), incorporating their different knowledge and experience.
1 Here, the term “professionals” refers to all personnel working in the facility or service, whatever their role or grade.
2 In this document, the term “process” is used to include: collection, analysis and consideration of the perspective of supported persons.
3 For a definition of “perspective”, see the box below: “Gathering perspectives - a multidimensional approach”.
4 This document can be useful in all types of processes to gather the perspective of supported persons, including in the development of a personalised support plan and children's support plan.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  6
Key points of the process
Gathering the perspectives of supported persons is the foremost principle of dignified care. In fact, expressing one’s opinion and preferences is a fundamental right, enshrined in numerous international conventions, including the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, as well as in various national laws. This right is guaranteed for everyone, without distinction of any kind (age, health, re-sources, etc.), so that people's living circumstances or situation do not prevent them from exercising it. This right applies to any person supported by a social or medico-social facility or service. Beyond the necessary compliance with the legal framework and respect for the supported persons themselves, expressing and gathering their perspective on the support provided by social or medico-social facilities or services is crucially important: the scientific literature clearly demonstrates the many benefits for supported persons, professionals and organisations.
Objectives
The main aim of gathering their perspective is to produce concrete, tangible actions that are beneficial for supported persons. Gathering the perspective of supported persons is an essential process that helps to empower them and therefore improve their quality of life and well-being. This process also helps improve the quality of work life of professionals and, more generally, the quality of the support provided and of organisational methods.
Legitimacy and expertise of supported persons
Supported persons are legal entities irrespective of their situation. Consequently, they have the right and the capacity to express their perspective on the support they are offered and to have these perspectives taken into consideration. As the main experts regarding their own lived experiences, sup-ported persons are the best placed to explain how they perceive their support, what they like or dislike and what they do or do not want.
Cooperation
The perspective of supported persons is paramount.
5
 The perspectives of the other persons in-volved (professionals, relatives, authorities, etc.) are complementary to, but do not take precedence over or replace, those of supported persons. The whole process is based on a collaborative and dy-namic approach, taking into account individual evolutions and promoting opportunities for cooperation with all stakeholders (supported persons, professionals working in the facility, family and friends, part-ners, funders, etc.). This requires the visible and lasting involvement of governance (board of directors, management, executives) to give legitimacy to the process and mobilise all stakeholders.
Methodology
The perspective gathering process uses validated methods and tools adapted to the different characteristics of supported persons. It is essential that supported persons can express their per-spective in a climate of trust and safety, without fear of judgement as to the validity or veracity of their perspective.
6
 It is essential to inform supported persons of the objectives of the approach and to en-courage them to participate in and take control of the entire process. The people who gather the per-spectives of the supported persons must be made aware of the approach, supported and engaged in this process.
5 Except in strictly defined circumstances, in particular related to potential endangerment.
6 Communication includes two dimensions: the ability to understand others and one's environment (comprehension) and the ability to express oneself (expression). (1)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025 7
 Gathering perspectives - a multidimensional approach
In this document, perspective concerns the experience, satisfaction and results ex-pected by people with respect to their support or care. (2, 3, 4, 5)
7
Although they are often confused, experience and satisfaction are two clearly separate things. This confusion stems from the similarity of the questions put to people, even though their purposes and methods of interpretation differ. In fact, experience can be defined as all the interactions and situa-tions experienced during support (the organisation and practices of social or medico-social facilities or services, relationships with professionals, family and friends, and peers, people’s life histories). Collecting this information makes it possible to express and explain lived experiences via two di-mensions:
-Functional: accessibility, comfort, response time, coordination, professional competency,etc.
-Relational: interpersonal interactions, emotional support, respect for choices, participation,involvement of family and friends, etc. (6)
Collecting satisfaction data can be useful for providing general management data on the activity in social or medico-social facilities or services, but its measurement is considered to be limited by a lack of scientific consensus. (3) Assessing satisfaction is complex, since it depends on individual expectations and preferences, which can lead to widely varying levels of satisfaction, even under equivalent support conditions. In addition, measurement tools often generate very high scores, lim-iting the identification of areas for improvement.
In sum, satisfaction measures what people think about their support or care on the basis of their individual preferences and expectations, i.e. the “what”, while experience goes beyond opinions to explore the “how” and “why” behind them.
8
 Examples:
-Satisfaction: do you feel safe here?
-Experience: Is there is a professional that you can talk to about your concerns and fears? Ifso, how can you contact them?
7 The numbers in brackets correspond to the bibliographic references indicated at the end of the document. For the literature search strategy, see the evidence report.
8 At present, research relating to the results expected by individuals has mainly been developed in the health sector. This theme will be the subject of future work aimed at the social and socio-medical sector.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025 8
1.Why collect perspectives?
Key points
Gathering the perspective of supported persons offers a number of advantages for all stakeholders: the supported persons themselves, professionals and organizations, as well as public policy and research players. These advantages are only possible if a set of ethical considerations are met (meaningful participation and repeated, freely-given and informed consent). These ethical considerations are part of an approach that promotes social justice through the concepts of self-determination, empowerment and epistemic justice. Recognition of individuals, their autonomy, their capacities, their experience and their knowledge helps to give supported persons better opportunities to lead a dignified and fulfilling life. These concepts also help to guide and structure professional thinking and practice.
1.1. Advantages of the collection process
Supported persons, professionals and organisations form a dynamic ecosystem in which everyone participates in the co-construction
9
 (4, 5, 7, 8) of a data collection process, based on mutual recognition of the experiences and expertise of each, for the benefit of all.
The perspective gathering process implies:
-Valuing the voice of supported persons as a central component in shared decision-making; (9-11)
-This shared decision-making provides opportunities for negotiation and power-sharing. (12)
Hearing the perspective of supported persons improves the quality, relevance and effectiveness of the services received (13-30) through better knowledge of the experiences, needs and expectations of the people supported by professionals (13, 14, 19, 21, 30-36), as well as services that are more closely aligned with the needs and expectations of the people in the organisations. (21, 22, 25, 27, 28, 37-40) The diagram below illustrates certain advantages for stakeholders:
9
 Haute Autorité de santé. Expérience patient et savoir expérientiel : deux notions à clarifier pour renforcer l’engagement ou la parti-cipation.
[Patient experience and experiential knowledge: two notions to be clarified to reinforce engagement or participation.] Edu-cational guide. Saint-Denis La Plaine: HAS; 2025 [pending publication].

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  9
1.1.1. Advantages for supported persons
When this process becomes part of people's daily lives over the long term, it has a significant impact in terms of autonomy and self-esteem. It also helps to reconfigure power relationships, promote citi-zenship and contribute to greater social inclusion.
The fundamental right of supported persons to express their perspectives is essential if their dignity is to be preserved. (41) In reality, maintaining dignity goes beyond the protection of physical integrity or in the face of other threats. (42) It also encompasses each person's ability to be fully involved in their own life, taking into account their past, present and future history. Hearing their perspective also directly contributes to this maintenance of dignity. (43-50)
By gathering their perspectives directly, the process encourages greater autonomy and empower-ment of people to act on their own behalf and on behalf of others. This process of empowerment operates on two levels: firstly, individually, through the acquisition of a greater awareness of rights and their effective implementation; and secondly, collectively, through the possibility of influencing changes in social or medico-social facilities or services. By giving access to a recognised position of expertise, it leads to a transformation in the relationship with these facilities and services. (20, 34-36, 42, 51-67) Some authors draw on the concept of agency, which they define as the capacity of individuals to influ-ence their own situation and make meaningful choices in their lives. This capacity is put into practice when people make decisions or participate in choices that affect them. (20, 21, 26, 68-72)
The opportunity to talk about their lived experience is therefore essential. Collecting their perspective helps to build or rebuild a positive sense of identity and improve self-esteem. Supported persons report an increased sense of legitimacy and personal worth when they are involved in decisions that affect them. This process can be explained by the collective recognition of expertise, which helps to counterbalance the detrimental effects of the vulnerability and stigmatisation that people can experi-ence. (20, 21, 26, 28, 43, 44, 52, 55, 59, 61, 62, 65, 73)
Active participation of individuals in decision-making processes, by expressing their perspective, cre-ates new opportunities for social connections. Spaces for collective and individual expression can thus encourage meaningful interaction and more horizontal relationships between peers and with profes-sionals, helping to reduce feelings of isolation or exclusion. (18, 21, 31, 38, 55, 57, 59, 63, 74-76)
These new relational dynamics help to improve communication between professionals, supported persons and their loved ones. They reinforce relationships of trust and mutual respect. They also promote confidence in the health system and contribute to greater uptake of support and care services. (9, 10, 14-16, 19-21, 29-32, 34, 50, 55, 64, 77-87)
By actively participating in the perception collection process, people have the opportunity to develop or reinforce knowledge and skills, particularly psychosocial skills, which can then be transferred to various aspects of their daily lives: communication, negotiation, critical thinking, situation analysis, problem solving, etc. (21, 27, 37-39, 52, 59, 60, 62, 73, 87-92)
By focusing directly on the aspects of support that are most important to supported persons, and on their needs and expectations, the perspective gathering process enables concrete improvements to be made to the quality, relevance and effectiveness of the services received, leading to services that are more closely aligned with the needs and expectations of the people within the organi-sations. (13-40, 52, 74, 75, 78, 93-95)
In conclusion, gathering and taking into account the perspective of supported persons helps to signif-icantly improve their support experience, their quality of life, their well-being and their satisfac-tion with their support. (13-29, 32, 55, 69, 86, 89, 90, 96, 97)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  10
 Recovery-oriented care
Recovery in mental health is defined as a personal and unique process aimed at enabling each individual to feel better and lead a meaningful life, despite mental illness or social vulnerabilities. It is based on several fundamental principles: hope, self-determination, social inclusion and empow-erment. It focuses on strengths, aspirations and resources, rather than just difficulties and peer sup-port. It fits squarely with the process of gathering and considering the perspective of supported persons. As part of this process, a recovery-oriented approach means recognizing supported per-sons as actors in their own lives. This contributes to the transformation of professional prac-tices and public policies by promoting a support relationship based on the direct expression of the perspective of supported persons, recognition of experiential knowledge and the co-construction of solutions. (75, 98-104)
1.1.2. Advantages for professionals
The collaborative approach encourages individual and collective learning, providing opportunities to exchange ideas and perspectives with colleagues and supported persons. (105, 106) It enables pro-fessionals to develop their knowledge and skills, particularly in terms of listening and gaining per-spective on their practices, (107, 108), cultural competencies (16) or participative processes. (28) They also gain a better understanding of the experiences, needs and expectations of supported per-sons. (13, 14, 33) In addition, the process encourages reflection on stereotypes and prejudices (58, 109) and consideration of supported persons beyond their difficulties. (110) Professional practices evolve through the incorporation of this approach into daily routines. They move towards a more rela-tional and less task-oriented approach. This makes it possible to form deeper, ongoing connections with supported persons. (111) Consequently, the process improves the quality of interactions with sup-ported persons (21, 29) and results in more appropriate and effective interventions. (31, 32) This ap-proach then leads to a better match between the needs of supported persons and the services provided, (112) which can result in greater consistency between the professional activity and the prin-ciples that underpin it (10, 113) and lead to a renewed sense of purpose (39) and a more rewarding working environment. (84)
1.1.3. Advantages for organisations and public policies
Supported persons provide relevant and accurate information and data about the support they receive, and serve as an important lever in enhancing the value of professionals’ work. Their involvement in decision-making processes helps improve professional and organisational practices. (9-11, 114) By giving all stakeholders the opportunity to freely express their needs, expectations and feelings, the competent authorities can better understand the key factors for improvement, as part of a partici-pative and co-constructed approach. (115) At different levels of the organisation (Social Council, Board of Directors, other forms of participation, etc.), people's experience becomes a valuable source of in-formation for improving the integration and continuity of the support and care offered. In fact, supported persons “can work with managers or professionals, for example, to co-construct continuous quality improvement projects”. (7)
The perspective of supported persons can provide useful information for the development of public policies, so that they are aligned with the needs, expectations and real living conditions of in-dividuals. (116, 117) From the design phase right through to evaluation, this participation is essential

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  11
in order to make the methods and tools more precise and better tailored. (118) The results of this collection process are important factors in the efficiency of public policies (67) and can be used to formulate concrete recommendations. (57) When carrying out their inspections, supervisory authorities can draw on the perspectives of supported persons to understand and assess the functioning of social or medico-social facilities or services. (119)
Another form of approach is the participation of supported persons in research projects. Their perspec-tive makes it possible to improve public health interventions (33) and increase the relevance of re-search and results (57, 60, 61, 69, 120-123) while valuing and giving meaning to their experience. (33, 61-63, 124) It also stimulates research and innovation in professional practices, which can lead to a cycle of continuous improvement and the adoption of new approaches. (125) In this context, supported persons can also learn and develop new skills and knowledge, which contributes to the positive devel-opment of their personal identity, self-awareness, and communauty belonging. (21, 37, 38, 59-63)
1.2. Ethical issues involved in perspective gathering
1.2.1. Promotion of social justice
Social justice is a fundamental principle that aims to reduce inequalities between people, by ensuring that every individual has access to the essential rights and resources necessary for their well-being. Gathering the perspective of supported persons helps to promote greater social jus-tice, particularly in a context where:
‒ Supported persons may not feel they have the right to share their perspective, think that it will not be taken into account or fear reprisals against themselves or professionals. In addition, they may feel that they are being questioned about issues that do not directly affect or interest them, and therefore do not wish to get involved; (126)
‒ Professionals frequently receive meaningful feedback from supported persons during everyday conversations, but these are not systematically incorporated into a structured perspective gath-ering and analysis process. (126)
Gathering the perspective of supported persons in order to promote social justice is rooted in the con-cepts of self-determination, empowerment and epistemic justice. Self-determination enables individ-uals to shape their own lives and make their own decisions based on their own values, preferences and choices, free from external influence and interference. It concerns all areas of life and is a marker for each individual of the expression of their citizenship. (127)
Complementing this concept, empowerment “refers to a person's perception of whether or not they have power and control over their life”. (127)  Some authors also draw on the concept of capabilities, which refers to the capacity to act, the “power to be or to do”, which implies taking into account the real freedom that people have to lead a life that they feel is worth living according to their own preferences and aspirations. (128-132) In the context of social or medico-social facilities or services, the develop-ment of empowerment (or capabilities) is linked to adaptations in the environment, including, in partic-ular, families and friends and professionals. (127, 133)  Organisations and professionals therefore play an important role in empowering supported persons. (133-136)
Acknowledging the autonomy and capacity to act of supported persons helps to achieve greater epis-temic justice, (137-139) i.e. to “take into consideration the knowledge and experience” of supported persons “in order to propose solutions that more closely match their needs”.  (140)  Epistemic justice fosters the expression of individual perspectives, along with listening to and consideration of these perspectives. (137-140)
Recognition of individuals, their autonomy, their capacities, their experience and their knowledge con-tributes to giving supported persons better opportunities to lead a dignified (133) and fulfilling life. These concepts also help to guide and structure professional thinking and practice.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  12
Beyond the use of these concepts, a set of ethical conditions is required to prevent any instrumentali-zation of the perspective (141) or purely symbolic participation of supported persons, also known as tokenism. (55, 58, 59, 142-146) The ethical conditions required are:
‒ Meaningful, relevant and beneficial participation of supported persons at all stages of the collection process, including systematic feedback to them on their contributions and on envis-aged actions; (13, 14, 147, 148)
‒ Consent (2, 15, 17, 25-30, 32, 35-38, 40, 48, 49, 52, 56, 59, 73, 74, 79 , 86, 90, 120, 124, 147-158) (written or verbal) that is freely given, informed and repeated during the process. It is im-portant to consider the following elements:
• People must be given clear explanations about the collection process and the use of the data collected;
• The explanation and the consent form (where this document exists) must be appropriate and accessible. For example, translated into the language of the person concerned; (152)
• Consent is dynamic and can change over time or depending on circumstances. A person may consent to a given activity at a given time and then withdraw their consent later, or they may consent to some aspects of an interaction but not others;
• Individuals have the right to remain silent, to limit the information shared and to withdraw from the process at any time;
• The perspectives expressed by supported persons belong to them throughout the process. At any time, they may request that the perspectives they have expressed be removed from the data collected or not be used.
These ethical considerations frame the collection process as an integral and fundamental part of the overall support and care framework, grounded in respect and concern for the individual, rather than as a one-off event in the quality process. (113)
 Respecting privacy and personalisation
Reconciling personalised support with respect for privacy is not always easy. Particular care needs to be taken not to create, even unintentionally, a kind of injunction on people to share their story. Some people may feel obliged to share private details of their lives believing that is what is expected of them. This implicit pressure can be a source of unease and run counter to the funda-mental right to privacy. (48, 49, 159-162)
1.2.2. Ethical questions
Gathering perspectives raises ethical issues at the various stages in the process (development, imple-mentation and feedback):
‒ How can protection of a person’s privacy be guaranteed when collecting personal information?
‒ What types of methods can be used to ensure that dignity is preserved?
‒ How can we avoid any form of obligation to participate, particularly for people in very vulnerable situations?
‒ How can it be guaranteed that consent really is freely given and informed?
‒ How can we avoid creating expectations that cannot be met?
‒ How do we provide feedback to someone who has made direct requests?

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  13
These questions are a real opportunity to reflect collectively on complex situations, to take a step back and look at practices from different angles, and to give meaning to everyday life. Spaces can then be created for collective reflection, both for supported persons and for pro-fessionals. (13-15, 17, 25, 26, 28-30, 32, 35-38, 40, 47, 52, 55, 56, 58, 59, 64, 73, 74, 77-79, 86, 87, 90, 92, 103, 105, 111, 120, 124, 142-148, 150-158, 163-178)
When collecting their perspective, supported persons may identify sensitive situations that go be-yond the initial scope of the process (serious events, dangerous situations, etc.) and/or report com-plaints indicating names (professionals, peers, etc.), etc.
In such cases, responses must be provided in accordance with the legal framework. They can also raise ethical questions in terms of practices, values and individual limits, which need to be examined:
‒ How do we reconcile respect for confidentiality, the duty to report and preservation of the rela-tionship of trust formed with the supported person?
‒ To what extent should the people tasked with collecting the information intervene or react in response to expressions of distress or unhappiness?
‒ What attitude should be adopted when revelations of serious events or dangerous situations implicate one or more colleagues?, etc.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  14
2. How can we work together to build a perspective gathering process?
Key points
For the collection of authentic and meaningful perspectives, it is important to establish a climate of trust by adapting spaces and postures, demonstrating respect and the im-portance attached to individuals and their perspectives. Gathering people's perspective must lead to concrete action and real changes in practices, organisation and even public policy. In order to have a positive impact on people's quality of life and on professional practices, this process requires the sustained involvement of all stakeholders. Govern-ance must be the central driver of the process and provide support for its teams.
2.1. Who voices their perspectives?
Scientific data show that everyone is able to express their perspective, as long as the methods, tools and conditions for collecting the data are appropriate, (76, 82, 105, 111, 118, 125, 172, 179-196) with a few exceptions indicated below. They also show that it is necessary to gather people's perspective directly, adapting the methods as far as possible. Using a third party to speak on behalf of the supported person (relatives, professionals) does not provide reliable data. (44, 74, 76, 79, 82, 105, 111, 118, 125, 172, 179-192, 197-201) Two situations constitute an exception to this rule:
‒ Individuals who do not wish to take part in the process and whose wishes must be respected;
‒ People for whom no method or tool has been found to communicate with them
10
.
2.2. Recognising experience, capacities, knowledge and expertise
The postulate of credibility (167) is an important principle that recognises the legitimacy of the per-spective of any supported person and considers their expression as reliable. (91) Even people whose ability to communicate is severely hampered (neurocognitive disorders, certain disabilities, allo-phones
11
, very young children, etc.) have unique knowledge that it must be possible to collect. (201) It is therefore important not to exclude certain people immediately on the grounds that they are unable to express themselves. In general, communication skills are maintained, even among elderly people with advanced illnesses or people with multiple disabilities. (2, 184) It is essential to be able to reflect on one's own thoughts and actions. (81, 177) The opportunities people have to express perspective depend on professionals' perception of their capacities and knowledge(204): that is why it is important to recognise the intrinsic value of the words and expertise, as well as the capacities, of directly sup-ported persons.
Acknowledging this principle means being vigilant about certain biases, such as acquiescence or social desirability bias. (72) These can be manifested when participants demonstrate submission
10 In this case alone, recourse to somebody close to the person may be considered. As long as they know the supported person very well and are able to interpret their lived experience. (52, 153, 155, 202, 203)
11 The term allophone is used here for people whose language of communication is not French and who have difficulty expressing themselves in that language.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  15
(asymmetry in the power relationship) or have difficulty understanding the questions. (167) Power im-balances between supported persons, their relatives and professionals can also lead to a risk of con-trol or self-censorship. (76, 172)
Certain groups of people are more vulnerable and their perspective may be less well docu-mented. For example, research (57, 205) has demonstrated that women in very precarious situations seek to make themselves invisible in order to protect themselves. This lack of visibility leads to a lack of scientific data, policies, programmes and support dedicated to such individuals.
 Plural experiential knowledge
Research has shown that people develop experiential knowledge, particularly during their care and support pathway. This knowledge is plural and can be illustrated as follows: (206)
Fig.1: Classification of patient knowledge by source of learning
2.3. Ensuring mutual confidence and understanding
A relationship of trust is essential when gathering perspectives. The aspects that contribute to this are:
‒ Thinking about the ethical, emotional, material, etc. conditions for collecting information, to give people the confidence they need to feel comfortable expressing their perspective. Pro-vide a space - chosen by the person, if possible - that is quiet and pleasant and where they feel safe; (38, 59)
‒ Demonstrating to people that their voice is valued. Experiences of marginalisation and pow-erlessness within the system can make it difficult to establish trust: some people may be used to not having their opinion sought or taken into consideration, and find it difficult to adapt to forms of interaction based on equality. (65)  It is therefore essential to create conditions in which the voice of the individual is fully recognised and valued; (71)
‒ Giving people the opportunity to control the process so that they can answer freely with complete confidence: what they are going to be questioned about, by whom, where, when, etc. Respect participants' right to remain silent, to limit the information they share and/or to withdraw from the process at any time; (26, 28, 36, 90)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  16
‒ Allowing people to determine for themselves the image they wish to project, which is why it is important to ensure participation arrangements designed to avoid compromising their dignity and self-image; (172)
‒ Being person-centred, (207) i.e. seeing the person before their difficulties (36) and listening without judging (19, 25, 28, 31, 40, 108, 207-209), which promotes trust and free expression;
‒ Showing respect in all circumstances: responding to requests (calls, letters, etc.), showing courtesy and punctuality are practices that build trust. (173)
 Example of a group charter constructed by people experiencing homelessness (59)
- Give each other space—To talk or to be quiet, whatever they might need.
- Be empathetic—Show this in your language and interactions. Respect our stories and other survey participants’ stories. Everyone's got a voice and freedom of speech, but this doesn't excuse racism, sexism, homophobia, transphobia or other types of discrimination.
- Think about your tone when you are speaking—try to change it if it sounds judgemental, con-descending, attacking, etc
- Help each other out—We all have our struggles and strengths. We all have good days and bad days.
- Be open minded—Show this in your language and interactions.
- Stop yourself from speaking over others or cutting others off—Let people finish or wait for a pause
-
Listen to others
Confidentiality—Don't discuss other stories outside of the group, don't discuss people full names outside of the group unless they give you consent for this.
2.4. Jointly defining implementation methods
While kind listening is essential, it may not be enough. The aim is to involve people in the whole process of gathering their perspective, but also to disseminate their messages in an accessible way that is likely to bring about changes in people's everyday lives, professional practices, the organisation of social or medico-social facilities or services, and even public policies. (63, 108, 124, 144, 210-212)
At first sight, this approach may appear to be costly in terms of resources, but the resulting benefits are well worth the commitment. To achieve this, it is not necessary to undertake a vast, exhaustive study of every aspect of people's lives. It is more a matter of clearly defining what it is important to collect and/or what people would like to share. It is also important to identify how the data collected can be used in practical ways in the lives of people and in the organisation of social or medico-social facilities or services.
This work needs to be a long-term process, in order to:
‒ Test different methods and tools depending on the context and objectives of the exercise;
‒ Create a climate of trust (in terms of relationships, material, etc.) with people so that they feel they have a legitimate right to express their perspective and are not afraid to do so;
‒ Create a dynamic with professionals so that they do not experience this process as a judgement of their work or a new constraint, but as an opportunity to better understand and take action;
‒ Provide tangible arguments to the various stakeholders: governance, friends and families, pric-ing and control authorities, etc.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  17
2.5. Creating conditions conducive to effective communication
It is important to ensure that each person understands the information, which means adapting the means of communication and vocabulary, particularly for people who cannot express themselves ver-bally. (11)  It is advisable to use accessible and inclusive forms of communication, in particular by drawing on the following advice. A number of aspects need to be adapted, depending on the individual needs of each person (55):
‒ Use of appropriate reading material and communication tools;
‒ Clear, jargon-free communication;
‒ Inclusion of supported persons in the reflection on the impact and integration of contributions;
‒ Flexibility and patience;
‒ Incorporation of non-verbal language and visual messages;
‒ Continuous information updates and feedback;
‒ Remain reflective;
‒ Sensitivity to terms used (cross-cultural situations, trauma, violence experienced, etc.);
‒ The questions asked must be concrete, descriptive and specific, short and formulated in the present tense, etc. (52, 89, 94, 156, 213)
Consider the use of non-verbal communication methods.
There are numerous ways of communicating adapted to the specific needs of individuals. For example, images (illustrations, photos, etc.) provide an opportunity to express what may be difficult to put into words and to enrich participants' verbal descriptions so that they are better understood. (150) Even infants are capable of communicating their intentions: they are active learners, display intentional be-haviour and know how to explore their environment. They use different modes of communication: ges-tures, vocalisations, body movements, facial expressions. (214)
 Augmentative and Alternative Communication (AAC)
The person whose perspective is being collected must be able to use their preferred means of communication to express themselves. (52, 65, 79, 153, 215)  If this communication method is non-verbal, the collection process can be adapted, using Augmentative and Alternative Communication (AAC). The aim is, firstly, to replace or supplement verbal communication and, secondly, to supple-ment or support the comprehension of people who have difficulty understanding verbal communica-tion. (52, 79, 153, 202) In addition to verbal communication, other forms of communication can be used simultaneously (52, 79, 153, 216), including:
‒ Gestures, sign language;
‒ Facial expressions, the use of eyes and body movements;
‒ The use of different tones, pitch or volume of voice or vocalisations;
‒ Machine-generated speech (computer, mobile device, etc.);
‒ Real objects, objects of reference, pictures and photos;
‒ Symbols, pictograms, etc.
Provide information
It is crucial that people have access to adequate and appropriate information throughout the pro-cess. This information must relate to the process (players, content, timing), the issues at stake and the options surrounding decision-making, as well as the way in which supported persons can express their disagreement with the decisions taken. (11)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  18
When preparing to collect perspectives, it is important to check that the questions and instructions are accessible, appropriate and understood; (51, 52, 89, 148, 154, 156) for example, with the cog-nitive interview, (89)  used in surveys to check participants’ understanding of the questions. This makes it possible to ensure that questions are understood and relevant.
Offer real opportunities for people to express themselves
To guarantee real opportunities for people to express themselves, it is essential to involve them from the start of the process. The relational aspects are fundamental: the real point of view only emerges once a positive relationship has been forged. Taking into account the time required to set up the per-spective collection process enables the right conditions for expression and interaction to be created. (11, 50, 68, 91, 173) Getting to know one another means taking the time to diversify ways of meeting and interacting. (50, 148, 215) During interactions, it is important to respect the extra time and breaks that people need to process information. (52, 94, 148, 167)
The Lundy model
Among the various models that exist relating to the notion of participation, the one drawn up by L. Lundy proposes four essential elements to ensure the effective participation of children and young people. These are briefly presented here:
- Space: children must be given safe, inclusive opportunities to form and express their view;
- Voice: children must be facilitated to express their view;
- Audience: the view must be listened to;
- Influence: the view must be acted upon, as appropriate, and children must be provided with feedback ex-plaining the reasons for decisions taken. (50, 66)
Space
Voice
Influence
Audience

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  19
3. What should be collected?
Key points
Gathering the perspective of supported persons should cover a wide range of aspects in order to take into account the complexity of their support experience. It is crucial to adopt a personalized and dynamic approach, focusing on what makes sense for each individual. The process must enable people to express themselves on all aspects of their lives that are important to them. Finally, it is essential to ask people why they value certain things, in order to prioritize issues and areas for improvement, in other words: the what, the how and also the why.
3.1. Defining together
It is essential that the entire process be constructed with the supported persons from the outset – starting with the selection of themes, through to the development of tools, and up to the presentation of the results. For the process to be meaningful, it is necessary to ask what truly matters and holds value for them. The aim is to understand, with them, what is really important and matters to them in their support and their everyday life, (9, 19, 24, 25, 36, 39, 43, 77, 93, 103, 107, 117, 118, 147, 181, 186, 188, 191, 192, 197, 203, 209, 217-223) and then to be able to explain why. (9, 25, 43, 44, 117, 118, 147, 157, 186, 188, 191, 192, 203, 217, 219-221, 224-226)
It is also essential to ask people to explain and specify why these elements are important to them. (9, 25, 43, 44, 117, 118, 147, 157, 186, 188, 191, 192, 203, 217, 219-221, 224-226)  Involvement from the outset ensures the relevance of the themes addressed, as well as engagement and participation throughout the process. This collaborative approach will make it possible to obtain results that are accurate and representative of their experiences and needs, and to work with them to take concrete action to improve living conditions, professional practices and organisational methods.
 Opt for differentiated methods of involvement
It is preferable to start the process by bringing together the different types of people (supported persons, professionals, family and friends, etc.) separately, and then gathering them together to find common solutions that are acceptable to all. (172, 227)  For example, to make it easier for elderly people to express themselves at a social council meeting, the agenda can be prepared in advance by them, with the support of a professional with whom they feel particularly comfortable.
It is not possible to learn everything about people's experiences in one go, because their sup-port is complex, multidimensional and involves several professionals in different situations, at different times and in different places. (64, 169, 172, 174, 182, 228)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  20
3.2. Typologies to support the reflection process
Given the need to decide with supported persons which themes they would like to focus on, it is difficult to define them in advance. However, an analysis of the literature reveals some useful typologies for discussion and reflection
12
. In the scientific literature, in terms of quality, the authors often refer to the work of Donabedian, (229) who makes a distinction between:
‒ Processes, i.e. activities or tasks carried out with or for people (functional and relational);
‒ Structures, i.e. organisational characteristics (level of equipment, number of professionals, level of qualifications, etc.);
‒ The results for supported persons.
Supported persons pay particular attention to interpersonal processes, especially the quality of rela-tionships (professionals, peers, family and friends). (9, 11, 13-19, 24, 25, 29, 31, 34, 36-39, 43, 44, 50, 64, 77, 78, 91, 93, 103, 116, 118, 120, 151, 157, 169, 173, 181, 186, 188, 191, 192, 203, 208, 209, 213, 217, 219, 221-223, 226, 230-237)
While interpersonal relationships are particularly important, this does not mean that structural aspects are unimportant. There is a minimum level of requirement below which material and living conditions, the quality of infrastructure and other factors become essential. (15, 19, 24, 35, 38, 39, 53, 67, 77, 78, 93, 103, 147, 151, 166, 197, 202, 208, 213, 230, 232, 237, 238)
The studies analysed distinguish between different themes (non-exhaustive list, without prioritisation):
‒ Subjective experience and everyday lived experience; (107, 181, 186): their daily life and their activities (11), their needs, expectations and aspirations; (211) their perspectives and pro-jections for the future; (25) their quality of life and subjective well-being; (67, 197)
‒ Social relationships and interactions: (50, 91, 173) the quality of relationships with profes-sionals; (44, 235) relationships with peers and the social environment; (53, 234) the feeling of being listened to, respected and treated with dignity; (93, 103)
‒ Support and care: planning and implementation of support; (11) personalisation of care and support; (118) respect for privacy; (103) assessment of support and perceived results; (9, 230) participation in decisions; (77, 157) access to, use of and satisfaction with services; (37, 222)
‒ Environment and living conditions: housing conditions and physical environment; (166, 208, 239) comfort, cleanliness and safety, (77, 230) fittings and equipment in premises; (103) sound and visual environment; (202)
‒ Autonomy and participation: the opportunity to make choices on a daily basis; (103, 197, 239) participation in meaningful activities; (43, 203) involvement in decisions; (13) the feeling of being considered as a person in their own right; (118, 186, 239)
‒ Social inclusion and citizenship: the feeling of belonging and social participation; (24, 230) access to education and employment; (208, 232) leisure and activities in the community; (77, 213) experiences of discrimination or stigmatisation; (31, 35)
‒ Information and communication; communication of comprehensible and appropriate infor-mation; (103, 166) access to information on rights and obligations; (147) understanding of rea-sons for decisions taken; (157)
‒ Coordination and continuity: the continuity and coordination of care and services; (78, 93) transitions between services or facilities; (215) access to and use of different services; (24, 38)
12 The elements below were discussed and reworked to correspond to scientific analysis categories. These are not necessarily terms, themes or categories directly derived from the views expressed by supported persons.

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  21
‒ Cultural and identity-related aspects: respect for linguistic, cultural and gender diversity; (103, 213) religious and spiritual practices; (147, 208) maintenance of identity and dignity; (191, 221)
‒ Safety and risk management: feeling of safety; (15, 147, 239) management of crisis situations or aggression; (231) use of restraint measures. (103)
 Beyond the services and facilities
Gathering people’s perspective on their support is not restricted to aspects or activities that take place in the facility or in the context of support at home. For example, school is a major dimension in the social, cognitive and emotional development of any child or young person, particularly for those who are protected. It can be particularly interesting to get them to talk about the way in which the social or medico-social facility or service supports them in their schooling. (240)
3.3. For effective implementation
It is preferable to proceed in stages. This ensures that everyone understands each other and the aims and conduct of the process. It also ensures that the results obtained are accessible to all and that they can be used to change people's living conditions, practices and organisational methods. (64, 169, 172, 174)
Use a shared language
Throughout all the stages, it is important to ensure that the language used is a shared one. Although common moments are experienced in the same places, the interpretative frameworks may be different. The terms, notions and even the concepts used may not have a clear meaning for everyone: being an actor in their own life, preserving dignity, etc., are very abstract notions for many people. That does not mean that supported persons do not have a point of view, but rather that they do not necessarily have the same codes or references for expressing themselves on this subject.
Start with an everyday situation
It is preferable to start from a concrete situation or moment that seems essential to the supported persons: their arrival at or preparation to leave the facility or institution; visits from relatives; cultural and leisure activities; medical appointments, etc. Some situations may seem trivial, anecdotal, surpris-ing, or even doubtful, which is normal, because the day-to-day experience is different depending on a person’s position (supported persons, professionals, family and friends, peers, volunteers, etc.).
Specify significant elements
Supported persons will then be able to specify what is particularly significant and important during this phase or this moment in their support: the people present, their level of intervention or otherwise, the places, the period, the way it is organised, etc. It is important to discuss what is facilitating and what is not, what is reassuring and what is not, etc. If they refer to a moment that seems trivial, it is useful to ask them to explain why they made this choice. Perhaps the full extent of what is at stake for people at that moment has not been identified. As some authors explain: the little things in life can have a big impact on quality of life. (44, 168, 169) Or perhaps it is a trivial moment, but one they have chosen to reassure themselves because they are afraid they will be unable to meet expectations or answer ques-tions properly. The literature reports the importance of the “here and now”: young children make deci-sions based on what they feel, what they think and what they want to do in real time, in response to concrete, live events. This may also be the case for some adults and children with disabilities. (52, 202, 241)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  22
Design questions
The various questions to be asked should be decided upon with the supported, persons, making sure that they are accessible and relevant to as many people as possible. The more precise this stage is, the more efficient and appropriate the collection tool and its questions will be. This is an opportunity to compare the perspectives of the different people involved, particularly supported persons and profes-sionals.
Add explanatory questions
Once the theme of the collection process has been chosen and the questions have been defined, it is important to include questions that allow people to explain and clarify their answers (the why).
 The importance of transitional phases
The literature suggests that transition support should be seen as a separate phase requiring specific planning and assistance. These transitions can lead to stressful, even traumatic situations that disrupt many identity-related and social reference points (entry into an institution, becoming an adult, arrival in a new country, access to transitional accommodation, etc.).
These observations highlight the need to gather the perspective of supported persons during and after such transitions. (24, 54, 64, 70, 81, 89, 151, 181, 202, 212, 215, 232, 233, 242-245)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  23
4. How are perspectives gathered?
Key points
Various methods exist. Alongside quantitative methods, qualitative methods and, more specifically, narrative and visual approaches, are often the most appropriate ways of gath-ering the perspective of supported persons. These methods are adaptable, inclusive and creative, depending on the needs and preferences of supported persons. They can also incorporate different communication methods and media to encourage interaction, save time and improve efficiency. Collecting a person’s perspective is not intended to be a therapeutic exercise or serve as an aid for interpretation. The sole objective is to gather perspectives. Those collecting the information need to be aware of, and even trained in, the symbolic and material conditions of the support provided.
4.1. Who collects perspectives?
The person collecting the perspective may be a third person in the support relationship: professional, peer, social council member, volunteer, researcher. Whatever the case, they must be trained in the chosen collection technique and aware of the specific characteristics of individuals and the support provided. (2, 36, 52, 54, 55, 76, 84, 96, 105, 111, 153, 157, 168, 170, 171, 230, 246)
4.1.1. Professionals
It is possible to get professionals involved, as long as they do not work directly with the persons ex-pressing their perspectives. (2, 76, 105, 111, 170, 171, 246) To achieve this, professionals from differ-ent departments can be rotated or partnered with another facility. All categories of professionals can be called upon: receptionists, night supervisors, care assistants, care auxiliaries, educators, nurses, legal representatives, housekeepers, assistants and foster carers, managers, etc.
4.1.2. Peers and representatives of supported persons
Peer support
13
 has developed considerably in France and in other countries and current studies tend to demonstrate that it has numerous positive impacts for the healthcare system. (20, 248-252)
14
Based on a model of egalitarian support, (254) it designates the engagement of people who have developed in-depth expertise in their support or care pathway, who wish to share their knowledge, skills and experience and become actively involved in the healthcare system. (247, 255)
The scope of peer support is vast and offers numerous intervention possibilities. It can take the form of support, mentoring, coaching, representation, research or training. This diversity of applications re-flects the richness and flexibility of this approach. People involved in peer support can be referred to by a variety of terms (peer supporter, patient partner, expert patient, peer health mediator, peer re-searcher, peer trainer, etc.). (22, 38, 60, 62, 256-258)
In the context of perspective gathering, peer supporters can have a positive impact throughout the process: (55, 87, 89, 96, 204, 210, 259) establish a relationship of trust, (55) improve the sense of
13 This presentation is likely to evolve following work undertaken by the HAS to study the topic of peer support. (247)
14 Here, precautions are taken because the most recent studies show that peer support constitutes a category of very varied intervention approaches that cannot be evaluated as a single entity. In fact, each type of intervention may have specific charac-teristics, objectives and impacts that require separate analysis and evaluation. (253)

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  24
safety and comfort, (55, 89, 96, 239) encourage different forms of expression, (55, 79, 210) clarify questions, (89) create information documents accessible to participants, provide keys for understand-ing and analysis(79), etc.
It is also possible for supported persons to participate in the perspective gathering process. However, it is advisable to prefer third parties who are not involved in day-to-day relations with their peers. This precaution can be explained by the potential complexity of interpersonal relationships between sup-ported persons. Certain situations could compromise confidentiality or free expression during the per-spective collection process. (38, 242)  As a reminder, it is essential that the people collecting the perspectives are both trained in the chosen technique and made aware of the precautions required depending on the specific characteristics of the people expressing themselves. It is possible to contact the facility’s social council (CVS), the regional council for supported persons (CRPA), or the depart-mental council for citizenship and autonomy (CDCA) to find peers and/or representatives.
4.1.3.  Researchers and students
Researchers are professionals who are qualified to provide methodological support without this nec-essarily involving a cost for the social or medico-social facility or service. Universities and research laboratories, particularly in human and social sciences or public health, are likely to welcome the op-portunity to collaborate (participative research, student internships).
In recent years, research has also been developing numerous projects in partnership with the people concerned, in particular those receiving support and their peers, and proposing new methods and techniques. (87, 260) Researchers, together with the people concerned, can identify gaps in knowledge so that together they can design resources that integrate lived experience and the results of research, underpinned by theory. (65, 87, 211, 212)  This makes it possible to produce more relevant and more useful resources for the target public. (87, 211, 212)
4.1.4. Volunteers
It is possible to mobilise volunteers to collect the perspective of supported persons (90, 168, 170, 218) and also to access certain populations (for example, refugees). (90)  Volunteers can be recruited in a variety of ways via the media and social networks, social and socio-cultural centres, word of mouth, etc. (261) However, particular care must be taken: volunteers must be trained and made aware of the specific characteristics of supported persons, the types of support provided, how the social or medico-social facility or service operates, the methods used to collect and analyse data, the impartiality of exchanges, etc.
4.2. The different methods
Diversifying the ways in which the data are gathered (using different techniques, at different times, etc.) gives a clearer picture of people's perspective. In addition, each method has advantages and limitations (see table below). Finally, in addition to the clearly identified times dedicated to collecting perspectives, the process will be more meaningful if it also includes regular, less formalised opportunities for ex-change, provided the person consents.
4.2.1. Individual and collective approaches
Perspective gathering can be conducted on two complementary levels: individual and collective. The individual approach gives supported persons the opportunity to take stock of their daily lives and to be more involved in decisions concerning their support. The collective approach, on the other hand, makes

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  25
it possible to have a group influence, thereby fostering the continuous improvement of services. (21, 70, 113)
Numerous studies combine both levels. (169, 170, 220, 262) Designing the perspective collection pro-tocol requires creativity, using unconventional qualitative methods (222) and multi-method and multi-media approaches. (52, 54, 148, 153, 155, 157, 215)  Participants can also choose between a group interview (focus group type) or an individual interview, depending on their preferences. (208)
While the individual approach is often favoured in interventions geared towards personal action, it is crucial to promote a collective approach. This enables a shared understanding and awareness of the issues, encouraging the search for mutual solutions. (113, 263)  It also encourages participants to rally around a common cause (176) and strengthens bonds within the group, while offering an alternative to face-to-face contact, which can sometimes be difficult for some people. (264)
4.2.2. Qualitative methods
Supported persons in social and medico-social services and facilities have very varied pathways and life situations. It is therefore difficult to apply a single method. In this context, qualitative methods can be particularly interesting. These methods explore attitudes, behaviours and experiences using meth-ods such as interviews, focus groups or observation. They can be adapted, adaptable, inclusive and even creative, depending on the needs and preferences of supported persons. Qualitative meth-ods can also combine several methods and means of communication and promote exchange between people. These methods enable a broader understanding of the experiences and influences that shape people’s life experience. (2, 21, 43, 52, 54, 80, 105, 121, 148, 153, 155, 157, 172, 190, 192, 208, 215, 222, 224, 265, 266)  In addition, qualitative methods save time and increase efficiency, since they provide access to an initial level of understanding of the perspectives collected, and even to identify possible courses of action. (43, 80, 105, 228)
The narrative approach
One of the qualitative methods frequently used is the narrative approach. (18, 21, 25-28, 35, 38, 39, 43, 56, 57, 68, 69, 86, 90, 107, 111, 150, 179, 180, 197, 208, 209, 213, 215, 267-269)  This approach is based on the principle that people's expression of their experiences plays a key role in their percep-tion of the world. It aims to analyse, without distorting, concrete situations and issues based on the perspectives of the people directly concerned. Using a collaborative approach, it makes it possible to understand and develop solutions that take into account the preferences and needs of supported persons. In this context, there is no biographic or therapeutic intention.
The approach offers a number of advantages for gathering people's perspective on their support ex-perience. For the people who express themselves: it allows them to share their experiences directly and to explain what is important and meaningful to them. By structuring their responses, it also gives them the opportunity to make sense of events and situations and gain a more complete understanding of their support. In this sense, it directly contributes to their empowerment and emancipation. For those collecting the information: the narrative approach gives them an opportunity, via active listening, (108) to understand the individual experiences, emotions and values that shape the way people experience their support. More specifically for professionals, it enables them to reflect on and analyse the influence of their own experience on their interactions with supported persons.
This is a flexible approach that makes it possible to adapt the interaction during the perspective gath-ering process, depending on the situation. It uses a range of different techniques designed to facilitate the involvement of supported persons (see table below).

HAS • Perspective of persons supported by social and medico-social services and facilities • May 2025  26
